(Note 1) Impairment losses
   (1) Goodwill
       For cash-generating unit groups to which goodwill is allocated, impairment tests are conducted at a specific time each year and
       at such time if there are indications of impairment. In principle, the Group considers the cash-generating unit groups to which
       goodwill is allocated as business segments.
       In impairment tests, the recoverable amount of the cash-generating unit groups is estimated and the carrying amount is
       compared with the recoverable amount. The recoverable amount of the cash-generating unit groups is calculated as fair value
       less disposal costs, by discounting estimated future cash flows based on business plans approved by the Board of Directors to
       their present value. In addition, the calculation of the perpetual value beyond the period of the business plan takes into account
       the long-term average growth rate of the market to which the cash-generating unit groups belong. These fair value
       measurements are categorized within Level three. The hierarchy used to measure fair value is described in “29. Financial
       Instruments (7) Fair value of financial instruments (ii)Breakdown of the fair value by level within the fair value hierarchy.”


       As a result of impairment tests in each consolidated fiscal year, the recoverable amount of goodwill is sufficiently higher than
       the carrying amount, and therefore, even if there are reasonable fluctuations in the important assumptions on which the
       calculation is based, it has been determined that the recoverable amount will not fall below the carrying amount, and therefore
       no impairment loss has been recognized. The outline of the main impairment tests for goodwill is as follows.
       (a) Americas pharmaceutical business
            Goodwill allocated to Americas pharmaceutical business mainly arises from the acquisitions of Morphotek, Inc. (U.S.A)
            and MGI PHARMA, Inc. The carrying amount of goodwill was ¥228,636 million (¥231,526 million as of March 31,
            2024).
            In impairment tests, the discount rate used to calculate fair value less costs of disposal is 9.85% of the weighted average
            cost of capital (9.01% for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2024) and the growth rate is 3.00% (3.00% for the fiscal year
            ended March 31, 2024).
       (b) China pharmaceutical business
            Goodwill allocated to China pharmaceutical business mainly arises from the acquisitions of Eisai (Liaoning)
            Pharmaceutical Co., Ltd. (China) and the carrying amount of goodwill was ¥3,048 million (¥3,084 million as of March
            31, 2024).
            In impairment tests, the discount rate used to calculate fair value less costs of disposal is 12.67% of the weighted average
            cost of capital (11.93% for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2024) and the growth rate is 4.00% (4.00% for the fiscal year
            ended March 31, 2024).


    (ii) Intangible assets
       The Group treats individual assets as cash-generating units for IPR&D assets (In-process research and development
       investments) and sales rights (exclusive rights to sell pharmaceutical product, etc.). For other intangible assets, business units
       that continuously monitor income and expenditures, or individual assets, are treated as cash-generating units.
       As IPR&D assets are intangible assets that are not yet available for use, impairment tests are performed at the same time each
       year and at the time there are indications of impairment. For sales rights and other intangible assets, impairment tests are
       performed on each reporting period end if there are indications of impairment.
       In impairment tests, the recoverable amount of the cash-generating unit is estimated and the carrying amount is compared with
       the recoverable amount. The recoverable amount of the cash-generating unit for IPR&D assets and sales rights is value in use,
       calculated by discounting estimated future cash flows based on business plans approved by management to present value. The
       discount rate used in calculating value in use is the pre-tax weighted average cost of capital.
       If the result of the impairment test in each consolidated fiscal year indicates that the value in use is less than the carrying
       amount of the cash-generating unit, an impairment loss is recognized. Impairment losses on IPR&D assets are included in
       research and development expenses, and impairment losses on sales rights are included in cost of sales. The main components
       of the impairment losses recognized are as follows:


       The fiscal year ended March 31, 2025
          The Company and BlissBio agreed that BlissBio will be solely responsible for future global development and
          commercialization of BB-1701, an antibody-drug conjugate jointly developed by both companies, and the Company
          decided not to exercise its option rights for a strategic collaboration based on the joint development agreement with
          BlissBio. As a result, in the fiscal year ended March 31, 2025 the Company recorded research and development expenses
          with the fair value of the related IPR&D assets as zero and the ¥3,740 million carrying amount of the IPR&D assets as




                                                                  - 167 -
                                                                                        Translation for reference purpose only

           impairment losses.


(Note 2) Significant intangible assets
       As of March 31, 2025, intangible assets mainly consist of sales rights acquired by acquisitions of companies or license or
       business alliance agreements for developing products, core technology, and IPR&D assets.


       Of the sales rights, the carrying amount of the sales rights for the orexin receptor antagonist Dayvigo (product name, generic
       name: lemborexant), acquired from Purdue Pharma L.P. (U.S.), is ¥12,602 million (¥13,698 million for the fiscal year ended
       March 31, 2024), and the remaining amortization period of the sales rights is 12 years. In addition, the carrying amount of the
       sales rights in Japan for the Janus kinase (JAK) inhibitor Jyseleca (generic name: filgotinib), acquired from Gilead Sciences
       K.K. (Tokyo), is ¥8,097 million (¥9,164 million for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2024), and the remaining amortization
       period for the sales rights is 8 years.
       The carrying amount of core technology related to the acquisitions of Morphotek Inc. was ¥4,386 million (¥6,617 million for
       the fiscal year ended March 31, 2024). The remaining amortization period of this intangible asset is 2 years.

 29. Financial Instruments
    (1) Capital management policies
         The Group maximizes corporate value by maintaining the integrity of its finance and flexibility, investing proactively for
      growth, and providing stable dividends. The key management indicator used by the Group is Net DER (net debt equity ratio),
      calculated as the ratio of net debt equity (equity attributable to owners of the parent).

    (2) Financial risk management policies
         In the course of conducting business activities, the Group is exposed to various financial risks (credit risks, market risks, and
      liquidity risks). Accordingly, the Group regularly monitors financial risks and implements measures needed to avoid or reduce
      risks, in accordance with internal rules, etc.
         The Group does not engage in derivatives transactions for speculative purposes.




                                                                     - 175 -
                                                                                          Translation for reference purpose only



  (3) Credit risks
       The Group extends credit to domestic and international business partners, thereby assuming the risk of breaches of contracts,
    etc. Regarding trade and other receivables, the Company regularly monitors the status of major business partners in each sales
    department and manages the deadlines and balances of each business partner, and strives to identify and mitigate, at an early
    stage, concerns regarding collection due to deterioration of financial conditions, etc. In addition, when using financial
    transactions, including derivatives, the Company engages with highly trustworthy financial institutions to reduce counterparty
    risk.
      The maximum exposure to credit risk for financial assets held by the Group is the carrying amount after deducting
    impairment losses as shown in the consolidated financial statements. However, there have been no significant impairment losses
     recognized in previous fiscal years, and there is no significant concentration of credit risk.
   (i) Changes in the loss allowance
       The changes in the loss allowance for trade and other receivables of the Group are as follows.

  (4) Market risk
       The Group is exposed to market risks such as exchange rate risks related to foreign currency transactions, interest rate
    fluctuation risk related to fund procurement, and market price fluctuation risk related to stock holdings, and therefore
    implements mitigation measures tailored to each risk.
   (i) Exchange rate fluctuation risk
       The Group conducts global business operations and faces the risk of sales transactions denominated in foreign currencies and
    net investments in foreign operations being affected by fluctuations in foreign currency.
      As a rule, the Group hedges the foreign exchange rate fluctuation risk of foreign currency-denominated trade receivables and
    payables by using forward exchange contracts. In addition, forward exchange contracts based on the status of exchange rates are
    used for planned transactions of foreign currency-denominated trade receivables and payables that are expected to occur without
    fail.


      Foreign exchange sensitivity analysis
        Foreign currency exchange risks arise from financial instruments denominated in foreign currencies, i.e., financial
      instruments in currencies other than the functional currency.
         If the currency of the foreign currency-denominated financial instruments held by each company in the Group appreciates by
      10% against their functional currency, the impact on the profit before income taxes of each company for the fiscal years ended
      March 31, 2025 and March 31, 2024, respectively, would be as follows.
         This does not include the impact of converting financial instruments denominated in the functional currency, assets,
      liabilities, revenues, and expenses of foreign operations into yen. It also assumes that currencies other than those used in the
      calculations do not fluctuate.
                                                                                                                       (Millions of yen)

                                                                    As of March 31, 2025                  As of March 31, 2024

Impact on the profit before income taxes (loss)

  USD                                                                                      (641)                                (2,870)

  EUR                                                                                    (2,265)                                (1,715)

  GBP                                                                                      2,600                                  3,973


   (ii) Interest rate risks
        The Group procures funds through borrowings from financial institutions and is exposed to interest rate fluctuation risk when
    procuring funds at variable interest rates. However, the Group mitigates this risk by using interest rate swap contracts to fix cash
    flows. Accordingly, the Group’s exposure to interest rate risks is limited, and the impact of interest rate fluctuations is minimal.
    All long-term borrowings at the end of the consolidated fiscal year ended March 31, 2025, are at fixed interest rates.


   (iii) Stock price fluctuation risk
        The Group holds stocks mainly to strengthen trading relationships or business relationships, and is therefore exposed to
    market price fluctuation risk. The Group regularly monitors market prices and the financial condition of issuers (business
    partner companies) to manage risks.
      Stocks are classified as financial assets measured at fair value through other comprehensive income and financial assets
    measured at fair value through profit or loss, but the impact of stock price fluctuations on other comprehensive income and
    profit or loss is minimal.

       Liquidity risk is the risk that the Group will be unable to make payments on their payment deadlines. The Group creates and

  (6) Derivatives transactions
       The Group uses derivative transactions to hedge foreign exchange rate fluctuation risks for foreign currency-denominated
    receivables and payables, and interest rate fluctuation risks for borrowings.
       Derivative transactions are executed by the Finance & Strategic Investment Department based on internal rules, while the
    Accounting & Tax Department handles office management and risk management. In addition, the department constantly reviews
    transaction balances and loss/gain on appraisal, etc., and reports the status to the assigned corporate officers as necessary.
   (i) Cash flow hedges
       The Company sometimes uses forward exchange contracts to hedge cash flow fluctuation risks of planned foreign currency-
    denominated transactions. In addition, interest rate swaps are sometimes used to hedge interest rate fluctuation risks for
    borrowings.
      The changes in the fair value of derivative transactions designated as cash flow hedges are recognized as other comprehensive
    income and are reclassified as profit or loss to offset the impact when the hedged item is recognized as profit or loss.
       The details of the fair value of derivative transactions for the fiscal years ended March 31, 2025 and March 31, 2024,
    respectively, are as follows.

  (7) Fair value of financial instruments
   (i)Fair value measurement
       Fair value measurement of the Group’s significant financial assets and liabilities is as follows.
    (a) Securities
          Securities consist mainly of listed securities. The fair value of listed securities is measured based on market values.
          The fair value of non-listed securities is measured by using the book value net asset method, multiple method and profit
       return method. In the multiple method, similar listed companies of the target company are selected, and the fair value of the
       target company is calculated using the stock index of the similar listed companies. In the profit return method, the cost of
       shareholders’ equity of the target company is used as the profit return rate, and the fair value is calculated from the profit
       amount of the target company. However, for investment in venture companies, the fair value is calculated based on the latest
      independent third-party transaction prices and the information on finance prices.
    (b) Derivative assets and liabilities
         Derivative assets and liabilities are measured using price information provided by correspondent financial institutions.
    (c) Borrowings
         The carrying amount of variable interest rate borrowings is deemed to be fair value as the interest rate approximates the
       market rate.
         The fair value of fixed interest rate borrowings is calculated by discounting the total amount of principal and interest
       payments by the interest rates that would presumably apply if similar borrowings were newly made.




                                                               - 179 -
                                                                                         Translation for reference purpose only

   (ii) Breakdown of the fair value by level within the fair value hierarchy
        The level of fair value measurement in the Group is divided into the following three levels according to the observability in
     the market.
          Level 1: Fair value is measured by quoted prices in active markets
          Level 2: Fair value is measured by using inputs other than Level 1 that are observable, either directly or indirectly
          Level 3: Fair value is measured by using unobservable inputs
       Breakdown of the fair value by level within the fair value hierarchy of financial instruments for the fiscal years ended March

 30. Lease
   (1) Lease activities (lessee)
         The Group’s lease activities as lessees mainly involve real estate leases for use as offices or research facilities. Many of these
      lease contracts include extension options for automatic contract renewal or cancellation options that can be exercised with prior
      notice within a certain period. The Group may exercise these extension and cancellation options in accordance with the
      Company’s real estate usage plans. When it becomes reasonably certain that the Group will exercise the extension and
      cancellation options, the right-of-use assets and lease liabilities are remeasured. There are no significant optional lease fees
      associated with exercising the extension and cancellation options.


    (2) Profit and loss related to lease (lessee)

    (c) The Company’s plan assets
          In the consolidated fiscal year ended March 31, 2025, the Company’s plan assets consist of plan assets managed by a
        corporate pension fund, and retirement benefit trusts, which account for 93.0% of the Group's plan assets.
           Management of plan assets in the Company’s corporate pension fund aims to maintain enough assets to maintain the plans
        through safe and efficient asset management to ensure the future payment of retirement benefit obligations. Accordingly, the
        Company thoroughly examines the allowable level of risk, determines the optimal asset allocation ratio within that risk range,
        and diversifies investments across various investment targets. The status of plan asset management is monitored quarterly by
        the Asset Management Committee, which revises asset allocations as necessary.
          The Company’s corporate pension fund recalculates contribution amounts every 5 years based on the end of the fiscal year
        to maintain financial equilibrium in the future. The fiscal verification of the fund’s balance is conducted annually, and if a
        shortfall occurs, measures such as revising the contribution rate are implemented.

 34. Share-based Compensation
   (1) Stock-based compensation system
            The Company’s stock-based compensation system for directors and corporate officers is as follows.
      (i)     Stock compensation system for directors
        Directors' compensation consists of fixed basic compensation only, a part of which is paid in shares. Stock compensation
      expenses are recognized based on the number of shares planned to be paid according to the term of office in the fiscal year
      ended March 31, 2025.


      (ii) Stock compensation system for corporate officers
          This system grants the Company's shares as compensation to corporate officers according to the degree of achievement of the
      Company's performance targets in each fiscal year.
        In the current consolidated fiscal year, stock compensation for corporate officers is recognized as stock-based compensation
      expenses based on the estimate at the end of the current fiscal year for the performance evaluation period. In addition, it includes
      the difference between the actual stock compensation paid in July 2024 and the estimate for the previous fiscal year, as well as
      stock compensation to be paid at the time of retirement.


        The Company allocates treasury shares through a third-party allocation, by resolution of the Board of Directors, to the
      Officer’s Compensation BIP (Board Incentive Plan) Trust account. Even after contributing to the trust, shares held in trust are
      accounted for as treasury stock. This stock-based compensation system is accounted for as equity-settled stock-based
      compensation.
            As of the end of the current consolidated fiscal year, the number of shares held in trust was 227,695 shares.


    (2) Share-based payments
                                                                                                                                  (Millions of yen)

 38. Subsequent Events
      On March 14, 2025, the Company decided to acquire the common shares and share acquisition rights of EcoNaviSta, Inc.
    (hereinafter referred to as “EcoNaviSta”) through a takeover bid (hereinafter referred to as “TOB”), which commenced on March
    17, 2025. EcoNaviSta then became a subsidiary on May 14, 2025 following the fulfilment of the conditions of the TOB. Through
    squeeze out procedures after the successful completion of the TOB, EcoNaviSta is planned to become a wholly-owned subsidiary
    in the first quarter of FY2025.


      (1) Name of the acquired company:
           EcoNaviSta, Inc.


      (2) Date of acquisition:
          May 14, 2025


      (3) Acquisition method of shares and stock acquisition rights
           7,031,940 common shares and 60,000 stock acquisition rights were acquired in consideration of cash.


      (4) Ratio of voting rights acquired
        97.1％


      (5) The primary reason for the business combination
          Based on the human healthcare (hhc) concept, the Company is promoting business activities towards building a dementia
       platform. Through this platform, the Company aims to support the prevention and early detection of mild cognitive impairment
        (MCI) and dementia in healthy people and people at high risk before the onset of these conditions. Additionally, the Company
        aims to support people after the onset of dementia to live their lives in their own way, not only through medication but also by
        providing other solutions such as communication apps and exercise programs.
           EcoNaviSta offers SaaS type monitoring services for the elderly, and their “Life Rhythm Navi,” which enables users to
        check the life rhythms of facility residents, is expected to become one of the core solutions in the Company’s dementia
        platform. The Company aims to create synergies and benefits by leveraging the strengths of both companies and achieve the
        prevention and early diagnosis of MCI and dementia by building an ecosystem in the dementia field, which is an urgent issue
        in Japan’s super-aging society.


      (6) Acquisition price
                                                                                                                                   (Millions of yen)

1. [Matters Related to the Basic Framework for Internal Control over Financial Reporting]
     Haruo Naito, Representative Corporate Officer and CEO of the Company, and Mitsuru Shomon, Chief Financial Officer of the
  Company, are responsible for establishing and operating internal control over financial reporting of the Company. The Company
  establishes and operates internal control over financial reporting in accordance with the basic framework for internal control as
  indicated in the document “On the Revision of the Standards and Practice Standards for Management Assessment and Audit
  concerning Internal Control Over Financial Reporting (Council Opinions)” issued by the Business Accounting Council.
    It should be noted that internal control over financial reporting may not prevent or detect misstatements in financial reporting
  due to inherent limitations of internal control, including errors of judgment or carelessness on the part of staff members and
  collusion by multiple staff members. In addition, it may not be possible to respond to changes in the internal and external
  environment of the organization or to irregular transactions that were not originally envisioned.
2. [Matters Related to the Scope of Valuation, Record Date, and Valuation Procedures]
(1) Outline of evaluation procedures for internal control over financial reporting
     The Company has assessed the effectiveness of the Company-wide internal control and the internal control over applicable
  business processes of the Company and its consolidated subsidiaries as of the record date of March 31, 2025, in accordance with
  generally accepted standards for the assessment of internal control over financial reporting.
     In this evaluation, the assessment of business processes subject to evaluation is conducted by taking into consideration the
  results of the evaluation of company-wide internal control, which has a significant impact on financial reporting on a
  consolidated basis. In the evaluation of these business processes, risks are assessed for each account item, and business processes
  related to high-risk accounts are analyzed to identify key control points that significantly affect the reliability of financial
  reporting. The effectiveness of internal control is then evaluated by assessing the design and operational effectiveness of these
  key control points.


(2) Scope of Evaluation of internal control over financial reporting
    The scope of evaluation of internal control over financial reporting for the Group is determined based on the materiality of its
  impact on the reliability of financial reporting.
    Materiality is assessed by taking into account both quantitative and qualitative factors, and the scope of evaluation for internal
  control over business locations and business processes is determined accordingly. In light of the “On the Revision of the
  Standards and Practice Standards for Management Assessment and Audit concerning Internal Control Over Financial Reporting
  (Council Opinions)” issued by the Business Accounting Council on April 7, 2023, and based on the results of prior evaluations of
  the effectiveness of internal control over financial reporting, the Company has revised its policy for determining the scope of
  evaluation, placing greater emphasis on a risk-based approach. Given that the Group’s core business is pharmaceuticals, revenue
  has been adopted as an appropriate quantitative indicator for assessing the significance of business locations other than the
  Company. As a qualitative criterion, in consideration of the importance of inventory, certain locations with global supply chain
  functions were additionally included within the scope of evaluation.
    As a result, Company-wide internal control evaluations were conducted for 17 entities, including the Company and other
  important business locations. For 32 entities deemed to have minimal impact on the reliability of financial reporting from a
  quantitative materiality standpoint, a qualitative evaluation was also conducted to determine whether any situations indicated
  potential emergence or changes in risk. As no material risks were identified, these entities were excluded from the scope of the
  Company-wide internal control evaluation.
    In determining important business locations, consolidated companies whose cumulative revenues after the elimination of inter-
  company transactions accounts, and are necessary to reach two-thirds of the total are considered important business locations.
     Thus, with regard to the relevant business locations, business processes for revenue, trade receivables, and inventories, which
  are accounts that are significantly related to the company’s business objectives, are the subject of evaluation. The Company
  identifies control points that have a significant impact on the reliability of financial reporting and evaluates the effectiveness of
  development and operation of such control points.
    Regardless of whether they were selected as important business locations, business processes related to significant accounts
  with a high likelihood of material misstatement and involving estimates and projections, specifically the valuation of goodwill
  and intangible assets and the valuation of deferred tax assets, are included in the scope of evaluation. Furthermore, the scope of
  evaluation includes the process for calculating profit shares associated with licensing and strategic alliances, as these are business
  processes that affect the Group’s operations and may impact financial reporting.
    If there are any items for which the results of the Company-wide internal control evaluation are not favorable, it is necessary to
  designate the related business locations as subject to evaluation. However, since the evaluation results of the Company-wide
  internal control for the current fiscal year indicated no material deficiencies requiring disclosure and were deemed favorable, no
  additional business locations were added to the scope of evaluation.



                                                                - 202 -
                                                                                     Translation for reference purpose only

     In addition, for business processes that have been excluded from the scope of evaluation over an extended period, we reviewed
  all relevant accounts from both quantitative and qualitative perspectives and determined that there was no need to include them in
  the scope of evaluation.
3. [Matters Related to Evaluation Results]
     Based on the results of assessment procedures, the Company has determined that its internal control over financial reporting as
  of March 31, 2025, is effective.
